> @nikitashilov9758 это совершенно не так. Форт Росс это всё, что англосаксонский кочевой сброд разрешил оставить. Настоящая русская Калифорния была огромной. Там были и верфи для строительства военных судов и торговые порты и поселения производившие сельхозпродукцию. Всё это коту под хвост когда нерусский царь решил поругаться с мексиканской империй из-за того, что они объявили независимость от Испании.   
>   
> В отличие от пришлых еврокочевников русские поселенцы не обижали местных индейцев. В музее Форт Росс выставлены специальные медальоны которые русские власти выдавали местным индейцам. На этих медальонах было написано что данный человек (!) является подданным Российской Империи и убивать его из-за скальпа нельзя. Иначе, американское правительство выплачивало своим гражданам премии за каждый скальп снятый с убитого индейца. Там тоже есть и прейскурант в музее - 5 центов за скальп взрослого индейца, 3 цента за женский скальп и 1 цент за скальп ребёнка.   
>   
> Когда американцы раскопали русское кладбище в Форт Росс, то были очень удивлены количеству смешанных семей где русские спокойно женились на местных и ещё привозили с собой смешанные с алеутами семьи. Все жили мирно и вместе тогда как еврошваль просто убивала местных и завозила чёрных рабов которых они за людей не считали.

Several misinterpretations follow after 13th minute:
1. Eastern expansion of Russia was driven primarily by the state, not because of misery. Yes, living conditions in Russia were always harsh, but Siberia was never a better place to live. So only adventurers and serving people would ever go there.
2. Ethnic cleaning was never a massive occurrence. It's actually what makes Russian expansion different from Anglo-saxonian expansion. Russians only need land to build for posts to protect themselves. Local people stay and live like they lived before. A lot of their descendants live there proudly till this day as full right citizens keeping their culture, language and having their own local governing instruments. There was no objective reasons to compete for resources because there was not so much people leaving there and a lot of landmass and forests. Some local people even joined the empire voluntarily, by peace and were never humiliated in their rights.
There were some clashes (and brutal ones, yes). It's normal process for any expansion. Some local people always resist, not because they have some particular state or economic interest, but just from princip, because they are afraid of strangers and do not want to have them near. But it was locals who attacked traveling eastward russians first, it was never an ethnic cleaning from russian side. First travelers were primarily traders, not soldiers. But random and painful attacks from local tribes made russian expeditions to bring protection for themselves and only then major clashes started happening.
3. Fatalism and pessimism are not the same. Russians (especially orthodox ones) are not pessimistic about eternal life. Only, may be, about mortal life (also not always). But a mortal life is not primal for Russians. And this is fatalism, not necessarily pessimism.

From 16th minute it's relatively accurate again.

On history part I would only add one thing because it's important: even though Peter The Great was the one who made Russia modern and military power, it's true, yet
1. territorial expansion started earlier;
2. at several points in history Russia managed to withstand military confrontations with western powers even before Peter The Great, but these confrontations were not so intence and loud as after him, indeed.

Music and literature interpretation is quite good yet debatable. I personally think there's more to it at some places, but the summary is good enough. And details are so complicated and also not so important for the topic, I think...

* what follows next is arguable because what people say in comments so mind it, but from what I know it have to be true *
The main thing you got to understand, why russians see this conflict as a threat to Russian civilisation is not only materialist part of the topic (even though it's important too), but more important - the threat of making their former brothers in spirit, the part of russian civilisation (Ukrainian people), making them westerners, - basically "killing their eastern slavic soul" by turning them to somebody else, somebody foreign.
It's like if somebody will come to your household and will manage to turn you parents or siblings against you, making them hate you.
It's more sacred then all of the wealth in the world. And this so called "west" took it from them (at least it's what it looks like for them).
THIS is what NO russian will ever forget or forgive, you can be sure of it. And this is the reason why Putin may be not the worst option in power. Because at least he is still open to talk, while many russians would already go va-bank, full scale war at this point...
Because the only thing more important for russians then their motherland is their soul.
And it is not matter does it make sense or not for you, - they believe it.
So while the picture is set like this, it does not matter who sits in Kremlin, the rage will only grow. Slow, but inevitable. Until the pressure to the east will fade.
It has accured before in history, and now it's happening again. There's no exit from it except getting rid of dominance mentality over Russia. Because russians will never accept it. They literally will fuel the resistance with everything they have untill they make sure nobody tries to overcome them anymore. Considering no time or price...

> ⁠@andrewkoshelev8765

> History is always a tricky subject. I often feel unsure about events before the 19th century, because I didn’t live through them, nor did my parents or grandparents. What we learn in school often feels like polished narratives, adjusted for each country’s benefit, and Ukraine is no exception.  
>   
> From my own experience, there was never real “hate” toward the Russian people as such. The frustration was directed toward governments that resisted Ukraine’s attempts to build a separate state. Even when I was a student, it was normal to grow up surrounded by Russian literature, music, and even YouTubers, since those cultural codes were strong and widely shared. By contrast, much of Ukrainian art was born out of repression and suffering, and often carried a fatalistic, tragic tone. Young people were naturally drawn to music and art with winning or uplifting narratives. Nationalism, until 2022, remained a small, marginal movement — but then, for understandable reasons, it became mainstream. Still, I don’t believe that building national identity on hostility toward neighbors is the best path forward.  
>   
> I don’t want to trace this only back to Hetmans and Cossacks, although the roots of Ukrainian separation and nationalism can already be found there. Historical facts matter, but they are never enough on their own. If we listen to the voices of Ukrainian writers — many of whom are barely acknowledged outside Ukraine — their suffering under the Soviet system is striking. Of course, poets everywhere often struggle under oppressive regimes, but in Ukraine the repression was tied specifically to language and identity. Ukrainian texts were dismissed as “village speech,” tolerated only in comic or folkloric form, while serious writing was expected in Russian. To succeed, you had to leave the village, learn another language, and hide part of your own identity. That suppression left deep wounds.  
>   
> There are sources that claim the Ukrainian language was restricted or banned dozens of times, and many who resisted were silenced, even killed. Soviet authorities persecuted not only Russians but also Ukrainians, particularly those who expressed even slight tendencies toward independence. Famines, like the Holodomor, were catastrophic, whether intended as deliberate repression or framed as part of broader Soviet policies. All of this fueled the fire of nationalism. The movement drew strength from writers of the “Sixtiers” generation and others who were killed, silenced, or forced into conformity.  
>   
> It’s important to note, too, that the idea of Ukraine as a separate state long predated the Soviet Union — it emerged after World War I, among other moments. The desire for independence was never an attack on Russia itself, but a natural response to political oppression. Far from being artificial, Ukrainian nationalism grew from lived experience.  
>   
> At the same time, I understand my great-grandparents’ perspective. They fought together with Russians during the world wars and embraced the Soviet ideal of unity. Many families were connected across republics, and they believed in this larger vision of brotherhood.  
>   
> That is why it’s so difficult to speak about these questions “objectively.” History is never neutral, because it’s always tied to emotions, perspectives, and agendas. What matters is how we interpret it today, without letting those interpretations justify new violence. In the current war, I have not seen a single reasonable argument that can justify the terror it has caused. Ordinary Ukrainians did not want this war, and it’s encouraging that Russians who protest against it show the same. Perhaps the true problem is not the people themselves, but the governments that play their own dangerous games.

### **@ahsverkoshkin** | 👍 244 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=Ugxif22_5YSuHEpmmyF4AaABAg)

“Slava” means “Glory” in Slavic Eastern European languages. It shows in older style (Pagan) names such as “Miroslav,” “Mstislav,””Ratislav,” “Bogoslav,” and so forth, where each name associates the bearer with glory in some of the things they can do to earn it, like fighting, being holy or bringing peace and prosperity. Think of it in the Norse Culture (Svear tribe, such as), where being a “Drengr” and developing such reputation — meant bringing glory and fame to yourself and your family/tribe. Which was part of the Drengskappr. Slavic people shared that kind of world view of doing exceptional things and gain the reputation by such actions, legacy... The first names were a bit like “magic” to help the person achieve something.

There’s absolutely no connection with the more modern word “Slave” and the pagan civilization, predating the Rome itself, that descended from Proto Europeans — Slavs. Of course they were not the only tribe with such roots, there was a wealth of people’s descendant from this ancient group.

Their (Slavic) cultural ties and religious practices — all have the Norse and proto-Iranian/Arian roots, such as some ideas shared among Zoroastrians, monotheistic believers predating all Abrahamic religions.

Next time I hear someone saying Slavs are named so because “slaves,” I would be triggered to make a long post again.

This comes from a historian and a linguist, studying ancient European and Northern European civilizations for… well, longer than I would like to admit.

Also, “lov,” in the word like “slovil,” means success in performing a hunt, fishing expedition, or other forms of hunting and or gathering. It’s only a hypothesis but there might be a direct correlation between “lov” and “slav,” that’s even more complex. The sounds of “O” and “A” often changed each other overtime. So the name of Slavs could literally mean being a successful hunter and gatherer peoples. Which in turn was bringing them the “glory.” Such linguistic interconnections are very common and in this case are very much possible.

In addition, Rus was Christened by 10th Century by Knyaz Vladimir “The Red Sun.” Before that, Rus was pagan, and so were a lot of Nordic tribes, at the time Slavs were sold at slave markets, they consisted of people of many world religions. People that caught other people to sell didn’t care that much about their religion. Such as the Polish Lithuanian Commonwealth (Christian) was capturing people from the modern day Western Ukraine for slave labor, being serfs, or slave markets, even though those people were likely Christians themselves.

I have a feeling that this man collected all the stereotypes about Russians and put them in a blender. Russian history is not only Orthodoxy, Vikings, Mongols, paganism and Tchaikovsky. The later an event or phenomenon occurred, the more it influenced the Russian mentality. Victories and suffering in World War II, disappointment in the West, Soviet atheism and respect for science, the criminal "code of honor" - this influenced the mentality of Russians more than religion and the events of the Middle Ages. Russians are different in different centuries, but their main features are best manifested in the main character of Tvardovsky's poem "Vasily Tyorkin" (or "Terkin"). I believe that Vasily Tyorkin is the most Russian in all of Russian literature. And Raskolnikov, the Karamazovs and other characters from the classics are fantasies of noble writers about their people.  The nobles were educated, but they saw the soul of the people through the prism of their elite upbringing.

> ​@gigimodo too much people with heads up there so - despite watching only 10 minutess of the lecture - I'll provide a remark on objectively wrong information which was provided even within those 10 minutes. So, on 7:14 proffessor Xueqin (I don't know his exact academic position, so I'll just be calling him like that) says that Moscow principality "lacked any natural defenses". In a grander scheme - yeah, comparing Moscow to UK or Japan - maybe, but the professor himself brought up Mongols, so it's not only logical to assume that he himself have been having them in mind as a main adversary for Moscow, but - also it is just historically correct to say that freeing themselves from Mongol rule - was the number one priority for Russian principalities at that time.   
>   
> So, that's the particular historical enemy of Rus at the time - Mongols. Their weapon of victory - is horse archers and constantly used "hit and run" tacticts (Mongols didn't consider tactical retreat as "dishonoroble" as European knights and Russian princes did). This tactic obviously requires steppe in order to work. Now - I advise you to go and look up the landscape of Southern Russia and Malorossia (Central Ukraine). Exactly, that's the steppe. And that's how Ancient Rus lost essentially its heartland to Mongols. Now - go and look up the landscape around Moscow. What you'll see - is thick forests. In summer, especially after heavy rains - those forests become literal jungles, impenetrable for any kind of riding army and impossible for mass archery-based tactics. And in winter - thick blanket of snow covers those forests also making them hard to traverse (not even talking about wolves). Also Moscovites created so called 'zaseka' - they cut down dozens of trees and left them laying in the middle of those "jungles". Would it still be possible for Mongols to reach Moscow if they really wanted to? Of course, and they did exactly that several times. Was it as easy as with Kiev or Chernigov? No, absolutely not, it was a strategic, logistical and tactical nightmare - surprise! - exactly because of the natural defenses. So this factor played a giant role in Moscow's rise to power in XIV century.   
>   
> So does it mean that - if professor Xueqin made such a mistake - that he had lost all credibility? No, of course not. I can say that he's mostly on point. After all, that's the only mistake I could find in 10 minutes. Does it mean that it's somehow outrageous to call him out on partial knowledge on the topic or quiestionable conceptualizing? No, absolutely not either.

### **@giakichanpan4022** | 👍 6 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=Ugyhe2lTLmmdh8l9i3R4AaABAg)

I don't know for which course this lecture was intended- if this is an extremely introductory course I could understand your approach - , but so far after the first 30 minutes (historical recap) I can say that nothing of depth was added. A series of general facts were cited, without providing any depth. This thick brush approach leads to wrong inferences which can be misleading. For example, you say that after the defeat of Napoleon Russia "is noticed" by the Western Europeans, ignoring all of the Russian interventions in Europe during the 18th century (Poland, 7 years war, russo-ottoman wars - which could have been a nice point from which to explain the situation in Ukraine-, French revolutionary wars...). The kievan rus and the mongols were mentioned but nothing useful was said of them. You skipped the "time of the troubles" and all of the dynamics involving the high ranking nobility and the Royal family (which could arguably explain the autoritarian aspect of the Romanovs) and you didn't mention the cossacks. Finally, you skipped half of the Russian 19th century and jumped from the 1812 war to the Great Game, which is just so wrong. 

In addition, from what I've read of 19th century literature (I'm not an expert admittedly) one of the main themes is the contradiction between technological and "liberal" progress and moral and physical misery, which could be argued is behind aome of the arguments behind the invasion of Ukraine. However I believe ideological arguments are only the superstructure of the Russian narrative, ultimately it was probably a cold and reasoned calculus, states are usually not suicidal. 

You could have touched on the history of the Orthodox church, and how it tried to bring pagans into the Christian faith by adapting their rituals and how it ended up fusing with the state apparatus.

What I drew from this was a lot of romanticism and little rational, historical explanations. I'm sorry for the criticism, but I believe more depth is needed to go beyond well-trodden traditional narratives.

With great respect to author and his interesting views, 
Please, do visit Russia and gather your own experiences in regards of national character and conditions.

Some of points were enlightening even to a russian, some points were factually wrong, but the most sad part, I think you missed the integral feeling of what russian mentality is. Theres a russian proverb, "couldn't see the forest behind all theese trees".

Saddest part for me were about violence, xenophoby and cleansings. They're just standard set of dehumanizing accusations. We're fatalist, and we do not trust easily, yes, but come and see for yourself our kindness and hospitality, please. All current situation is asymmetric, and westerner in Russia risks nothing, as long as he's obeying the law.

As for peoples relations, Russia, whilst harsh mistress to it's citizens, did not only let many ethnicities live and inhabit their forefathers lands, but also cared enough to let them have own culture, language and religion. Yes, there were exclusions (and each a tale of it's own), but as an exclusions they proofed the rule: any existing ethnographic video will show this. Hundreds of traditions to this day, alive and evolving. I beleive that integration without deconstruction is much humane and kind than what happened in Africa, or with Native Americans. 

I mean, if stereotype and facts contradict, why keep holding to stereotypes? It might cloud your judjement.

According to the theory of Alexander Samoilovich Akhiezer, the development of societies split in socio-cultural terms (and the Russian one is the most striking example of such) occurs in the form of cycles of inversions - a pendulum movement from one socio-cultural pole to another. The dynamics and values ​​(in general - the paradigm) of each pole is the opposite of the other.
The current stage is an obvious extreme movement of the pendulum in one direction. Reversal is inevitable. The fact that the natural decline in the popularity of Putin and his policies was interrupted and reversed by the Ukrainian events only compressed the spring even more.
Russian history is divided into segments, each of which is built on a sharp rejection of the legacy of the previous one, their pathos, their megatrend are fully opposite in polarity.
It seems that we are talking about different civilizational models within one country.

In 1812, there were two armies: the first consisted of Napoleon's soldiers and the soldiers of the Romanov Emperor in St. Petersburg. These two formed an alliance against Moscow. The second army consisted of soldiers from old Russia—the Muscovite Tsardom—and the Ottoman Empire—streltsy, oprichniki, Janissary Cossacks, and militias.

What was Napoleon's war? ---------- It was a theatrical production—a show—a grandiose spectacle necessary, without enormous resources (which Europeans never had), to convince as many people as possible that the Great European Napoleon had defeated the Great Russian Tsar of Tsars and his Ottoman army.

The scenario was good:
1. Moscow is burned and trampled.

2. The Third Rome of Moscow is taken.
3. The victors legally proclaim Their Rome – the Great Holy Roman Empire of the Holy German Nation.

4. All the hellish hassle and negotiations to organize the spectacle of "The Brilliant Napoleon Conquered Everyone" were worth it.

Alexander and Napoleon agreed that everything would be tip-top and smooth:

1. The blind, deaf, and aged Kutuzov agreed to the shameful Battle of Borodino.

2. Moscow is surrendered to St. Petersburg (the Romanovs), and the long-awaited colonial regime is established.

3. The Romanovs celebrate their triumph, gifting Europe with the former Ottoman Empire and the Kingdom of Moscow. Since they owe their throne to the Europeans and must repay... everyone is happy...
There are no dissatisfied Russians -- everyone has fled... victory is certain.

N-N-O-O-O-O, the Russian Spirit materializes here!
The Russian peasant didn't realize it was a "beautiful Moulin Rouge" and gave the actors in white pants a real beating. The cleverly conceived performance ended in utter failure. Alexander was forced to flee to Paris and give incredible gifts. Holy Rome was finally declared the Holy Land of the German Nation, but somehow it didn't work. And Bonaparte was sent away for disrupting a crucial operation. That's why monuments to this performance are erected 100 years later, when the shame has been forgotten. And I want to talk about Rome again.
And the monuments are terrifying and pointed to the Future—because the message is: Look and be afraid—We can give you such a terrible time that you won't know what hit you.

The shameful legend of the "invincible" Napoleon! Napoleon is the Europeans' first Zelensky... the same goals:
- 1. creating a legend,
2. building a "great" empire, a Germanic Rome... Ukraine... Kazakhstan, etc.
3. creeping in under an "elegant" pretext,
4. conquest and colonization.

.......everything with Napoleon ended in failure, despite his glorification, promises of empire in a non-existent empire, and lies about invincibility. The same will happen with the new "Napoleon-Zelensky." Old ideas don't work.
But there's a danger that the rulers of the world will do the same to the loser Zelensky as they did to the loser Napa - put him in a crystal coffin in the center of a large crypt and force all losers to pray to him and parade around him...that's how history is made...

Prof. Jiang – referring to your lecture Civilization 53 Dostoevsky and the Soul of Russia I would like to indicate that in Central and Eastern Europe there was the Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth (Rzeczpospolita Obojga Narodów). The state started its existence by the union between Poland and Lithuania on 15th February 1386.  Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth  officially began on 1 July 1569 with the signing of the Union of Lublin, which joined the Kingdom of Poland and the Grand Duchy of Lithuania into a single federated state. The Commonwealth ceased to exist in 1795, after the Third Partition of Poland, when the remaining territories were divided among the Russian Empire, the Kingdom of Prussia, and the Habsburg Monarchy. Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth   engulfed territories of present Poland, Lithuania, Belarus, Ukraine and part of present Russia. Feliks Koneczny (1862–1949) was a Polish historian, historiographer, and philosopher of history who developed an original theory of civilizations as semi‑autonomous “societal‑organismic” entities. His key work is the multi‑volume Studium nad cywilizacjami (“Study of Civilizations”), in which he argues that civilizations are distinct “methods of organizing collective life,” encompassing law, politics, economy, culture, and morality while remaining internally coherent and historically specific.
Core ideas of his theory
Koneczny distinguishes several major civilizations (for example Latin, Byzantine, Turanian, Arab, Brahmin, Chinese, and Jewish) and stresses that no single “universal” civilization exists; different civilizations follow their own internal laws and moral orders. He formulates a set of “civilizational laws,” such as the necessity of dominance when two full‑blown civilizations meet on the same territory and the tendency of civilizations either to coexist in conflict or to synthesize into new forms over time.
His view of Latin civilization and Poland
Within this framework, Koneczny praises the Latin civilization (including Poland, Italy, and Latin‑speaking Europe) for its “personalist” character, emphasizing the responsible, morally‑accountable individual rather than pure collectivism or individualism. He sees Poland as a key actor within the Latin‑civilizational orbit, mediating between East and West and thus playing a special role in preventing the domination of more “despot‑friendly” or “negational” civilizations.

Russia is not the successor of the Mongol Empire; it simply seized the former lands of that state. Russia is more of a spiritual successor to Byzantium. 

Despite the fact that Russia is home to many ethnic groups, 80% of its population is made up of ethnic Russians (European Slavs). A country where one ethnic group accounts for more than 50% of the population can be considered mono-ethnic. 
There are many such nuances.

### **@DenisSG1981** | 👍 3 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=UgyxJSGDqZdqSbvIjFN4AaABAg)

Not a single noticeable ethnic group was subject to cleansing in Russia. They are all still there. In many regions the local ethnic groups are majority. For foreigners we all look the same, but for locals it is well visible, where you have Russians, and where you have locals who speak Russian like a first language (and often also know and speak their native language). “Pictures” of “cleansing” is something completely else.
The expansion was often not peaceful, but no “ethnic cleansing” (see above about local majorities).
There are also many regions where Russians are majority, but in those regions if you count the number of villages with local ethnic groups it is very high. In large settlements assimilation was happening, but the small settlements did not disappear.
Keep in mind that we often speak about the regions with population 1-3 million which is by a factor of 2-10 larger than the area of France. One does not compete for the lacking space in such areas, one competes with winter frosts and swamps.
Of course, there were episodes of crimes and fights between local nations and locals and Russians, but all of that were episodes of criminal violence and stupidity, not a state policy of a general rule.

What is missing here is that Russia he fell in love with has very little to do with Russia of today. 19th Century culture, the subject of the lecture, was created by <2% of the population, and that thin cultural layer was either wiped out or subjugated during 74 years of the Soviet rule. He did not list Shalamov. He should have. He did not talk about the legacy of WW2 and what it did to the nation's psyche. He should have. Yes, the core features of the national character remain, but they operate at a very basic level. Resilience and perseverance as immune reactions to adversity are core values, but what do they mean today? Avoidance of hunger, avoidance of cold, avoidance of authorities, you die today so that I die tomorrow. Homo homini lupus est. Russia expanded East because it could - sparsely populated feudal lands did not offer a sufficient resistance to deter the process. Ukraine is doing it now btw.

### **@cyruskirill**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=UgztQehUVlwIXZLGglp4AaABAg)

You are damn right about the Russian music! It's so cool to see people being interested in it. 

You marked it with precision: "One's soul must suffer to write such a beautiful music". This is exactly what Chaikovsky is about. He suffered a lot in his life and even tried committing suicide (just right when writing the "Swan lake"). 

In the "Swan lake", even the melody itself narrates about it. The flute tries to rise, to fly away high above - just like a human soul (31:32). But it is always cruelly "grabbed" back to Earth by this dark, really evil move played by the brass (31:45)

Even the melody itself consists of two antagonistic parts. It starts in light H minor, trying to rise, like a little dove. But then it is always "grabbed" back by the dark G major, which sounds like a predator bird, a vulture

### **@srsatlas** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=UgyXJu0hl7QJtPcSWYx4AaABAg)

The English word “slave” derives from the Medieval Latin word sclāvus, which itself comes from the Late Latin Sclāvus, meaning “Slav.” This connection exists because many Slavic people were captured and enslaved during the early Middle Ages, particularly around the 9th century, when Germanic and other European powers frequently took Slavs as slaves in wars and raids.
The term sclāvus was borrowed into Old French as esclave, then into Middle English as sclave and eventually “slave.” This etymology reflects historical circumstances rather than the Slavic people’s own naming or identity. It is actually the other way around.

### **@oleglebedko4496**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=UgxRn9r3YZs_w6CVzix4AaABAg)

Great rundown of Russian literature, religion and mentality in such a short time format. I thought it would be the weaker part of the lecture, but it was pretty good.

There are a few points that are worth clarifying on history:
1. Conquering of the east and Siberia. Sure there were battles and hardship for local population. However, the locals were not enslaved, killed or removed from the land as was done by the Americans. They became vassals, but they kept the language, traditions, religion (including pagan ones), local governments and the land. That's why later you acknowledge yourself that Russia was "incoherent", and it's the same today. Unlike the US that was really ethnically cleansed in two major waves.

2. Burning Moscow by the Russians in 1812 is the French story. According to Russian historians, the French started robbing Russian churches, homes of aristocrats, looting, getting drunk etc. and in the process started the fire. Obviously, they don't want to admit such a barbaric behavior, so, as always the westerners do, they blamed it on the Russians. BTW, it was not just France, it was pretty much entire continental Europe. That's why it had the biggest army of all times before that - over 600,000 troops. 

3. Europeans bought Russian slaves in Venice and Ottoman Empire long after they become Christians. Many thousands of Russians were captured by Crimean Tatar raiders every other year in 15th-16th centuries, taken to slavery and sold, mostly to European "nobility". The raids were huge, fast and went as far as Kiev and Moscow.

4. Exit from the World War I was caused by Bolshevik ideology and propaganda, not the other way around. Though, of cause, the war related problems helped the rise of the Bolsheviks. There is indirect evidence that Lenin was working for Austrians. 

And yeah, I thought the Russians were just realists, given the brutal environment and history.

### **@Neurosong.**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=Ugwld6Nz1NT3DWPmCeJ4AaABAg)

Александр Твардовский
Василий Теркин: 4. Переправа
Переправа, переправа!
Берег левый, берег правый,
Снег шершавый, кромка льда…
Кому память, кому слава,
Кому темная вода, -
Ни приметы, ни следа.
Ночью, первым из колонны,
Обломав у края лед,
Погрузился на понтоны
Первый взвод.
Погрузился, оттолкнулся
И пошел. Второй за ним.
Приготовился, пригнулся
Третий следом за вторым.
Как плоты, пошли понтоны,
Громыхнул один, другой
Басовым, железным тоном,
Точно крыша под ногой.
И плывут бойцы куда-то,
Притаив штыки в тени.
И совсем свои ребята
Сразу — будто не они,
Сразу будто не похожи
На своих, на тех ребят:
Как-то все дружней и строже,
Как-то все тебе дороже
И родней, чем час назад.
Поглядеть — и впрямь — ребята!
Как, по правде, желторот,
Холостой ли он, женатый,
Этот стриженый народ.
Но уже идут ребята,
На войне живут бойцы,
Как когда-нибудь в двадцатом
Их товарищи — отцы.
Тем путем идут суровым,
Что и двести лет назад
Проходил с ружьем кремневым
Русский труженик-солдат.
Мимо их висков вихрастых,
Возле их мальчишьих глаз
Смерть в бою свистела часто
И минет ли в этот раз?
Налегли, гребут, потея,
Управляются с шестом.
А вода ревет правее —
Под подорванным мостом.
Вот уже на середине
Их относит и кружит…
А вода ревет в теснине,
Жухлый лед в куски крошит,
Меж погнутых балок фермы
Бьется в пене и в пыли…
А уж первый взвод, наверно,
Достает шестом земли.
Позади шумит протока,
И кругом — чужая ночь.
И уже он так далеко,
Что ни крикнуть, ни помочь.
И чернеет там зубчатый,
За холодною чертой,
Неподступный, непочатый
Лес над черною водой.
Переправа, переправа!
Берег правый, как стена…
Этой ночи след кровавый
В море вынесла волна.
Было так: из тьмы глубокой,
Огненный взметнув клинок,
Луч прожектора протоку
Пересек наискосок.
И столбом поставил воду
Вдруг снаряд. Понтоны — в ряд.
Густо было там народу —
Наших стриженых ребят…
И увиделось впервые,
Не забудется оно:
Люди теплые, живые
Шли на дно, на дно, на дно…
Под огнем неразбериха —
Где свои, где кто, где связь?
Только вскоре стало тихо, —
Переправа сорвалась.
И покамест неизвестно,
Кто там робкий, кто герой,
Кто там парень расчудесный,
А наверно, был такой.
Переправа, переправа…
Темень, холод. Ночь как год.
Но вцепился в берег правый,
Там остался первый взвод.
И о нем молчат ребята
В боевом родном кругу,
Словно чем-то виноваты,
Кто на левом берегу.
Не видать конца ночлегу.
За ночь грудою взялась
Пополам со льдом и снегом
Перемешанная грязь.
И усталая с похода,
Что б там ни было, — жива,
Дремлет, скорчившись, пехота,
Сунув руки в рукава.
Дремлет, скорчившись, пехота,
И в лесу, в ночи глухой
Сапогами пахнет, потом,
Мерзлой хвоей и махрой.
Чутко дышит берег этот
Вместе с теми, что на том
Под обрывом ждут рассвета,
Греют землю животом, -
Ждут рассвета, ждут подмоги,
Духом падать не хотят.
Ночь проходит, нет дороги
Ни вперед и ни назад…
А быть может, там с полночи
Порошит снежок им в очи,
И уже давно
Он не тает в их глазницах
И пыльцой лежит на лицах —
Мертвым все равно.
Стужи, холода не слышат,
Смерть за смертью не страшна,
Хоть еще паек им пишет
Первой роты старшина.
Старшина паек им пишет,
А по почте полевой
Не быстрей идут, не тише
Письма старые домой,
Что еще ребята сами
На привале при огне
Где-нибудь в лесу писали
Друг у друга на спине…
Из Рязани, из Казани,
Из Сибири, из Москвы —
Спят бойцы.
Свое сказали
И уже навек правы.
И тверда, как камень, груда,
Где застыли их следы…
Может — так, а может — чудо?
Хоть бы знак какой оттуда,
И беда б за полбеды.
Долги ночи, жестки зори
В ноябре — к зиме седой.
Два бойца сидят в дозоре
Над холодною водой.
То ли снится, то ли мнится,
Показалось что невесть,
То ли иней на ресницах,
То ли вправду что-то есть?
Видят — маленькая точка
Показалась вдалеке:
То ли чурка, то ли бочка
Проплывает по реке?
— Нет, не чурка и не бочка —
Просто глазу маята.
— Не пловец ли одиночка?
— Шутишь, брат. Вода не та!
Да, вода… Помыслить страшно.
Даже рыбам холодна.
— Не из наших ли вчерашних
Поднялся какой со дна?..
Оба разом присмирели.
И сказал один боец:
— Нет, он выплыл бы в шинели,
С полной выкладкой, мертвец.
Оба здорово продрогли,
Как бы ни было, — впервой.
Подошел сержант с биноклем.
Присмотрелся: нет, живой.
— Нет, живой. Без гимнастерки.
— А не фриц? Не к нам ли в тыл?
— Нет. А может, это Теркин? —
Кто-то робко пошутил.
— Стой, ребята, не соваться,
Толку нет спускать понтон.
— Разрешите попытаться?
— Что пытаться!
— Братцы, — он!
И, у заберегов корку
Ледяную обломав,
Он как он, Василий Теркин,
Встал живой, — добрался вплавь.
Гладкий, голый, как из бани,
Встал, шатаясь тяжело.
Ни зубами, ни губами
Не работает — свело.
Подхватили, обвязали,
Дали валенки с ноги.
Пригрозили, приказали —
Можешь, нет ли, а беги.
Под горой, в штабной избушке,
Парня тотчас на кровать
Положили для просушки,
Стали спиртом растирать.
Растирали, растирали…
Вдруг он молвит, как во сне:
— Доктор, доктор, а нельзя ли
Изнутри погреться мне,
Чтоб не все на кожу тратить?
Дали стопку — начал жить,
Приподнялся на кровати:
— Разрешите доложить.
Взвод на правом берегу
Жив-здоров назло врагу!
Лейтенант всего лишь просит
Огоньку туда подбросить.
А уж следом за огнем
Встанем, ноги разомнем.
Что там есть, перекалечим,
Переправу обеспечим…
Доложил по форме, словно
Тотчас плыть ему назад.
— Молодец! — сказал полковник.—
Молодец! Спасибо, брат.
И с улыбкою неробкой
Говорит тогда боец:
— А еще нельзя ли стопку,
Потому как молодец?
Посмотрел полковник строго,
Покосился на бойца.
— Молодец, а будет много —
Сразу две.
— Так два ж конца…
Переправа, переправа!
Пушки бьют в кромешной мгле.
Бой идет святой и правый.
Смертный бой не ради славы,
Ради жизни на земле.

### **@Rubinrus**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=UgwBoqfkPdpOF7_ehj54AaABAg)

Russian colonization practice has consisted of sending out cossacks and criminals, and also just serfs running away. There were some scimrishes with the locals, but those were limited to the start of the colonization (crushing the quasi-states in Siberia) and then some barbarism because, well, both cossacks and criminals sent there weren't exactly good people. However it was forbidden later on to clash with the locals as long as they pay the tribute and don't cause a ruckus because, like, cossacks and criminals aren't the best huntsmen and Siberia isn't known for having good living conditions. Basically it was like: 
1) Claim territory as Russia, crush anyone opposing this 
2) Prevent forces (tribes) from fighting and collect the toll for doing so
3) If the toll isn't paid - cossacks will raid you
4) If you want to continue speaking your own language, continue practicing your religion or whatever - none of Russia's concern. Just deliver the fur on time, don't fight among yourselves, don't rebel and then you can do whatever 

The main difference with the traditional colonization - no assimilation. Even the language - if you want to, you can learn it. If you don't - the positions within the government apparatus will be out of touch for you but that's about it. This practice started to become more "European" towards the end of 19th century and during 20th century with the Polish constantly rebelling and Ukraine learning from them. It's more or less similar with how Chinese Empire was turning people from whatever into Han - if you want to become "civilized", then you learn Russian and become Russian. 

The most brutal colonization effort so far came from USSR when the Soviets wanted to force the reforms in Siberia and Ukraine. The result of Ukrainian reforms is well-known, but results in Sakha (Yakutia) and Kazakhstan are often overlooked - they're about as terrible. Then, there was an effort to culturally assimilate Warsaw Pact into becoming the Third Romans (Soviets). 

Also there was a missed factor that Russia was isolated for the most of its history. While European countries were trading and by doing so, making technological and cultural progress, Russia was catching up in Great Leaps Forward. And when Russia has acquired European territories like Poland, instead of using it as a engine of progress they've limited its influence on the Heartland to the bare minimum. And then, during Nicholas II's rule, yet another Great Leap has failed drastically. And for the normal type of progress? - it doesn't work in Russia. It needs a hard push to commit to the Great Leap and risk it all.

"Slavic" comes from the word meaning "to praise" or "to glorify", originally referring to praising God, or in ancient times, the Gods, ancestor spirits, and Nature. 

"Slave," on the other hand, is a Latin word. 

"Slavyane" (the Slavic people) is not Latin, it may be of Greek origin or something else, but definitely not Latin. 

It's almost the same as if we called China "Kitay" (the Russian name for China) and then claimed it means "the country of people who live with whales", just because "kit" means whale in Russian.

### **@vladimir4164** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=UgyrVtkxymk-Tf_O6y14AaABAg)

At the end of the video, he called us racists? With so many nationalities (around two hundred) in Russia, how can we be racist? Even Africans live here now, and no one persecutes them on racial grounds. On the contrary, we have fought and continue to fight against this! Regarding the so-called eastward expansion, I also don't entirely agree, because the Russians didn't exterminate the local population completely, but rather tried to integrate the local population into the general Russian culture, helping and teaching them to organize their lives in a more civilized manner without interfering with local customs. This is a big difference from what America or Britain did.
There's also a contradiction in the word "slavic": it doesn't come from the word "slave" (rab), but from the word "slava" (glory), or some believe it comes from the word "slovo", but certainly not from "rab" (slave).
This is where the problems with understanding us Russians come from. The original approach and method of understanding are based on mistakes. How can someone who himself stated in a video that he doesn't fully understand Russian culture try to explain it to others? I advise anyone who wants to understand Russians to delve as deeply into their culture and history as possible, rather than trying to understand everything superficially or through stereotypes.


В конце видео он назвал нас расистами? Имея столько (около двухсот) национальностей в России как мы можем быть расистами? Да у нас даже африканцы сейчас живут и никто их не преследует по расовым признакам. Мы наоборот боролись и боремся с этим! По поводу так называемой экспансии на восток я тоже не совсем согласен т.к. русские не истребляли местное население под корень, а наоборот старались интегрировать местное население в общую культуру России, помогая и обучая местных обустраивать свою жизнь более цивилизованно при этом не вмешиваясь в местные обычаи. В этом большая разница с тем, что делала Америка или Британия.
Так же есть противоречие в слове "slavic" оно не происходит от слова "раб" (slave), а от слова "слава" (glory) или кто-то считает от слова "слово", но никак не от раба!
От этого и проблемы понимания нас русских. Изначальный подход и метод понимания зиждиться на ошибках. Thx great lecture. I would like to add only one thing - you forgot about soul (but put it in your title by the way😁). In Russian culture we have three parts of a human been - mind, heart and a soul (and it even can be reflection of a Christianity religion where we have Holy Trinity - the Father, the Son (Jesus Christ), and the Holy Spirit). So if "Anna Karenina" is much about heart, then "Crime and punishment" is much more about soul and less about heart. 

We have some idiomatic expression like - "глаза зеркало души" (The eyes are the mirror of the soul), "мечущаяся душа" (restless soul). The last one Is suitable to both novels and to all Russian literature and poetry of 19 century, especially in Dostoevsky's works.

### **@thule1823** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=Ugy6y8U44Jtup8fyfD54AaABAg)

Stopped at 13 minutes to say:  what he said about Russia's expansion eastward is just categorically untrue. First, it was actually mostly driven by the state - first Ivan Grozny taking Kazan, then by state-sponsored expeditions such as Ermak Timofeevich. People who wanted to escape and live on the fringe would become Cossacks on Don and Zaporozhye, in the south, they usually wouldn't go to Sibir.

The claims of "brutality" and "ethnic cleansing" are also plainly wrong. All of the Siberian peoples are still there, dozens of ethnicities - they still have their language, culture, religion (we have Muslims in Tatarstan and Buddhists in Kalmykia, Bashkiria etc), and autonomy. Many joined voluntarily.  Once you go far enough east, there is no grounds for competition and ethnic cleansing, because human settlements are so sparse and locals often aren't even agricultural people, they're hunters and gatherers. Russians would come, build a fortified city, work the earth, and locals would come to trade. Of course there were battles with local lords, of course there was violence, but it was downright peaceful compared to almost any other colonization anywhere. And certainly compared to how America was conquered.

### **@NikolaDimitriev-t6w**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=UgxfOIbH844-EiOa_5F4AaABAg)

Historians tend to emphasise the role of environment factors of the character development of a nation.

I think, that the real reason, why westerners don't understand Russians, is because western philosophy and thinking is fundamentally based on the Greek school and its derivatives. Greek thinkers needed to win arguments in public forums and be proven (more importantly be seen proven) right. This school of thought has little tolerance to paradoxes and points of view. Western style philosopher will sometimes over-simplify, rationalise or dismiss contradicting objective truths, until theory loses connection with reality, in order to formulate a simple law/explanation that satisfies mathematical logic.

Russian, like many other cultures, on the hand, delight in finding revealing contradictions. If a simple explanation for logical discrepancy is not found, Russians will verify the conflicting truths and accept the paradox as knowledge. "If I can't explain something, it doesn't mean that it doesn't exist. I take for true, what I see, until I have further knowledge on the subject.". It leads to superstition, but also to very healthy rational scepticism. Hence, the mysticism and fatalism. This is why it is easier for Russians to understand westerners, than it is for westerners to understand anyone other than themselves.

How Prussia started its existence? The biggest Polish geopolitical mistake (?)
At the end of 1225 or the beginning of 1226, the duke Konrad Mazowiecki asked Hermann von Salza, the Grand Master of the Teutonic Order, for help. In return for dealing with his pagan neighbors, he promised to grant the Order the Chełmno Land, located on the Prussian frontier, while reserving to himself the fullness of his ducal prerogatives. In March 1226, Emperor Frederick II sanctioned the Masovian duke’s grant, but at the same time he bestowed all of Prussia upon the Teutonic Order as a direct imperial fief. In practice this meant that on the conquered territories the Order would be able to establish its own state, entirely independent of Konrad I.
The first knights arrived on the banks of the Vistula in 1228. In the same year the Chełmno Land was transferred. Within two years Konrad I granted the knights further privileges. In 1230 more Teutonic brothers arrived. The Order was ready to deal with the pagans.

### **@96Vano**

Decent lecture. Lots and lots of mistakes and misunderstanding (see another comments), but unfortunately, we are somewhat get used to it. Everyone understands history differently.



First, we are not exactly imperialistic. All our history is about being surrounded by rivals that want our people, resources and land that we earned with great struggle and can't properly defend (geography is a b*tch). There were a dark parts in history, yeah, same as everyone had, we are not saints, but in the end of the day, all we want is a safe space. We don't need more soil. We are totalitarian, because we even abandoned some of our freedom to gain security. And for most parts we're totally okay with that. Russia is a safe country actually, in comparison with modern EU, and we know the prize of safety. Plz don't democratize us ;)



Second, about last question: fatalism and resilience. We have a popular saings, "Trust in God, but do your best" (На Бога надейся, а сам не плошай) and "The one who is on guard, is guarded by God" (Береженого Бог бережет). Rude translation, but meaning is there. Most of the times folklore gives good insight on nation. It's really something from north, I think. Only those who eager, determined, can have a god's help and mercy. And everything is on god's mercy.



Third, Ukraine. It's unfathomably complicated topic. And there is no one-sentenced answer for it. Can't speak for our politicians, but I spoke with a lot of people about it and most of them feels that situation is totally wrong, but somewhat inevitable. It's like younger brother fighting older brother after their parents died and older brother trying to be the head of the family, but youngster resisting him and making his entire personality around not being in this family and not realizing that without family he is literally nothing and no one cares for him and nowhere he will be at home. But the saddest part is that no one kills slavs better than other slavs, see Balkan's case, because we put our lives in what we believe is true and fight to the bitter end. That's why this war so brutal. We don't want Europe soil, we have more than enough for generations, we just want to keep our family intact.



And finally, some words from our poet Fedor Tutchev, that summing everything up greatly:



"One can't comprehend Russia with mind

Can't measure it with common rule:

It has its special trait - 

In Russia you can only trust" (similar words "trust-hope-faith")



"Умом Россию не понять,

Аршином общим не измерить:

У ней особенная стать —

В Россию можно только верить."



From Evil Empire with love ❤

### **@СлонМармон** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0rALqcPWuH0&lc=UgxWnzfenm5OTpHkKlp4AaABAg)

Dude, you lack basic knowledge of Russian history, and the nonsense you're spewing is just unbearable to listen to.

For instance, you claim Moscow was weaker than Novgorod, but the actual era of Novgorod's greatness was before Yaroslav moved to Kyiv, long before the Mongol-Tatar yoke. Moscow became the center of the Russian lands during the Mongol-Tatar yoke itself; Muscovite princes collected tribute for the Mongols from other Russian territories. In other words, Moscow became the administrative center. During that time, although Novgorod was a free city, it had already become a deep political province, and Kyiv had been completely destroyed—literally wiped off the face of the earth.

The Tatars didn't just 'leave' on their own; they were defeated by the unified Russian cities. It was a 250-year resistance. From 1223 to 1480, a massive battle occurred every 40–50 years. This culminated in the Great Stand on the Ugra River, after which the Tatars finally left, so to speak. But by that time, it was already the 15th century, and there had been no 'vacuum' in Russia for a long time—the Muscovite Principality had already annexed the Russian cities, including Novgorod, and unified the Russian state.

Russia never drove the indigenous populations off Siberian lands. There were no 'lands' in that sense; those people lived by hunting and trading fur. The Russians took control of trading forts where this fur was exchanged for goods. These forts were previously under Mongol control, and some didn't belong to anyone at all. But no one was driven out; to this day, Buryats live in Buryatia, Chechens in Chechnya, and Tuvans in Tuva.

Russians aren't ashamed of the Mongol-Tatar yoke, just as they aren't 'proud' of the Viking rule in Northern Russia.

The claim that Russians despise Asians, or that Orthodox Christians hate other branches of Christianity, is an offensive lie. Russia contains not only other branches of Christianity but also entire regions that are Muslim or Buddhist. In fact, the incitement of xenophobia is viewed by some as a product of external propaganda.

Before discussing civilizations, it would be beneficial to study history further and perhaps visit Russia a few times.
